## Document 009: 115年9月海關進出口貿易初步統計 (2026-10-08)

115 年 9 月海關進出口貿易初步統計 
一、進出口貿易變動 
（一）出口 
9 月出口 872.2 億美元，創歷年單月新高，較上月增 5.9%，較上年
同月增 60.9%(按新臺幣計算增 68.2%)；第3 季出口 2,449.2 億美元，
較上年同季增 44.6%；累計 1 至 9 月 6,615.5 億美元，較上年同期增
46.2%(按新臺幣計算增 49.1%)。 
（二）進口 
9 月進口 635.9 億美元，亦創歷年單月新高，較上月增 5.8%，較上
年同月增 51.7%(按新臺幣計算增 58.6%)；第 3 季進口 1,818.0 億美
元，較上年同季增 44.3%；累計 1 至 9 月 5,010.0 億美元，較上年同
期增 41.3%(按新臺幣計算增 43.8%)。 
（三）出入超 
9 月出超 236.3 億美元，為單月新高，較上年同月增 113.4 億美元；
累計 1 至 9 月出超 1,605.5 億美元，較上年同期增 625.4 億美元。

二、出口主要貨品 
（一）與上年同月比較 
由於創新科技應用商機持續擴展，推升相關供應鏈出貨動能，加上
消費性電子新品備貨需求與產品價格上漲效應之挹注， 9 月出口較
上年同月增 330.0 億美元(+60.9%)，主要變動如下： 
1. 資通與視聽產品出口 391.3 億美元(歷年單月次高)， 較上年同月增
198.6 億美元(+1.0 倍)， 其中電腦及其附屬單元增157.4 億美元(+1.1
倍)、儲存媒體增 17.6 億美元(+2.2 倍)、交換器及路由器增 10.5
億美元(+1.1 倍)。 
2. 電子零組件出口 311.3 億美元(歷年單月次高)， 較上年同月增97.1
億美元(+45.3%)，其中積體電路增 93.9 億美元(+46.1%)、印刷電
路增 1.4 億美元(+33.7%)、 電容器及電阻器增0.8 億美元(+32.0%)。 
3. 礦產品出口 22.6 億美元，較上年同月增 10.5 億美元(+86.4%)，其
中石油煉製品增 10.1 億美元(+99.0%)。 
4. 機械出口 27.4 億美元(歷年單月次高)，較上年同月增 6.4 億美元
(+30.7%)，其中生產半導體等機械增 2.7 億美元(+61.0%)、未列名
具特殊功能機械增 0.6 億美元(+73.6%)。 
5. 基本金屬及其製品出口 26.9 億美元，較上年同月增 5.0 億美元
(+22.9%)，其中銅及其製品增 2.5 億美元(+65.1%)、鋼鐵及其製品
增 1.4 億美元(+11.7%)。

（二）與上年同期比較 
1. 第 3 季出口較上年同季增 755.9 億美元(+44.6%)，以資通與視聽產
品增 367.6 億美元(+55.0%)、電子零組件增 308.4 億美元(+51.2%)
增加較多。 
2. 累計 1 至 9 月出口較上年同期增 2,089.8 億美元(+46.2%)，其中資
通與視聽產品增 1,222.2 億美元(+72.4%)、電子零組件增 726.6 億
美元(+45.7%)。

等出現結構性轉變，爰另予併計。

三、進口主要貨品 
（一）與上年同月比較 
隨 AI 產業鏈全球分工與出口引申需求擴增，電子零組件、資通產品
及半導體設備進口維持強勁，加上國際農工原料行情處於高檔，9
月進口較上年同月增 216.7 億美元(+51.7%)，主要變動如下： 
1. 農工原料進口440.5 億美元， 較上年同月增163.9億美元(+59.3%)，
其中電子零組件增 112.6 億美元(+82.5%)、電腦之零附件增 19.4
億美元(+1.5 倍)、礦產品增 17.1 億美元(+38.9%)。 
2. 資本設備進口 128.0 億美元， 較上年同月增 36.0 億美元(+39.1%)，
其中資通設備增 18.4 億美元(+75.0%)、半導體設備增 12.4 億美元
(+34.9%)，交通運輸設備減 1.4 億美元(-46.4%)。 
3. 消費品進口 62.1 億美元，較上年同月增 16.5 億美元(+36.0%)，其
中固態非揮發性儲存裝置增 16.4 億美元(+5.3 倍)、筆電增 0.5 億
美元(+43.8%)，藥粧用品減 1.2 億美元(-16.6%)。

四、主要出口國家/地區 
（一）與上年同月比較 
9 月對東協出口創新高，對美國、陸港為單月次高 
1. 對美國出口 274.6 億美元，較上年同月增 141.5 億美元(+1.1 倍)，
其中資通與視聽產品增 124.1 億美元(+1.3 倍)、 電子零組件增10.1
億美元(+1.3 倍)、機械增 2.2 億美元(+42.3%)。 
2. 對中國大陸與香港出口 206.2 億美元，較上年同月增 47.8 億美元
(+30.2%)，其中電子零組件增 34.9 億美元(+30.5%)、資通與視聽
產品增 5.7 億美元(+39.0%)、機械增 1.9 億美元(+43.7%)。 
3. 對東協出口 188.8 億美元，較上年同月增 91.2 億美元(+93.4%)，
其中資通與視聽產品增 49.7 億美元(+2.2 倍)、電子零組件增 32.0
億美元(+63.3%)、礦產品增 4.8 億美元(+1.4 倍)。 
4. 對歐洲出口 45.3 億美元，較上年同月增 7.7 億美元(+20.6%)，其
中資通與視聽產品增 5.7 億美元(+34.1%)、電子零組件增 2.2 億美
元(+56.6%)，基本金屬及其製品減 0.8 億美元(-20.5%)。 
5. 對日本出口 30.7 億美元，較上年同月增 3.6 億美元(+13.4%)，其
中電子零組件增 1.5 億美元(+11.0%)、基本金屬及其製品增 0.6 億
美元(+32.5%)、機械增 0.5 億美元(+29.1%)。 
（二）與上年同期比較 
累計 1 至 9 月對美國出口 2,077.8 億美元，較上年同期增 773.1 億美
元(+59.3%)，對中國大陸與香港出口 1,600.9 億美元，增 355.3 億美
元(+28.5%)， 對東協出口1,339.6 億美元 ， 增463.6 億美元(+52.9%)，
對歐洲出口 421.8 億美元 ， 增138.4 億美元(+48.8%)， 對日本出口277.7
億美元，增 58.3 億美元(+26.6%)。

五、主要進口國家/地區 
（一）與上年同月比較 
9 月自中國大陸與香港進口 135.0 億美元 ， 較上年同月增50.1 億美元
(+59.1%)，自南韓進口 116.3 億美元，增52.6 億美元(+82.5%)，自東
協進口 106.0 億美元，增 52.6 億美元(+98.4%)，自日本進口 64.3 億
美元，增 17.7 億美元(+38.0%)，自歐洲進口 50.2 億美元，增 3.5 億
美元(+7.4%)，自美國進口 48.1 億美元，增 6.5 億美元(+15.5%)。 
（二）與上年同期比較 
累計 1 至 9 月自主要國家/地區進口與上年同期比較 ， 自東協增77.6%、
自南韓增 65.8%、自中國大陸與香港增 42.8%、自日本增 25.7%、自
美國增 21.4%、自歐洲增 10.8%。

六、主要國家/地區貿易概況 
（一）主要國家/地區今年 1-8 月外貿景氣普遍走升，以南韓(1-9 月)出口年
增 56.8%最為強勁，香港、新加坡亦分別年增 49.0%、39.8%；中國
大陸、美國出口分別年增 19.3%及 14.6%，德國(1-7 月)、日本各增
9.7%、8.3%。 
（二）主要國家/地區進口同步上揚，累計 1-8 月以香港進口年增 50.5%較
突出，新加坡、中國大陸分別年增 37.3%、27.0%，南韓(1-9 月)、德
國(1-7 月)分別增 19.7%、10.0%，日本、美國各增 7.8%、4.2%。

### Page 7

7
圖 5 2026 年主要國家/地區出進口年增率
七、綜合分析
（一）受惠於人工智慧(AI)、高效能運算及雲端服務等應用商機持續擴展，
加以步入傳統外銷旺季 、產品價格上漲效應，9 月出口 872.2 億美元，
年增 60.9%，連續 35 個月正成長；進口635.9 億美元，年增51.7%，
主因 AI 產業鏈國際分工與出口引申需求擴張，及國際原物料行情居
高；出、進口值及出超 236.3 億美元齊創單月新高。累計 1 至 9 月出
口年增 46.2%、進口增 41.3%。
（二）9 月主要貨品出口普遍較上年同月成長，以資通與視聽產品增 1.0 倍
最佳，包括顯示卡、伺服器、儲存媒體、 交換器及路由器等出口均
呈倍數增加 ；其次為礦產品增 86.4%，主因全球油品供應偏緊支撐價
格維持高檔；電子零組件隨 AI 應用及消費性電子新品備貨需求升溫，
增 45.3%，機械、電機產品亦各增 3 成上下。累計 1 至 9 月資通與視
聽產品、電子零組件出口併計增 59.5%，占總出口比重 79.0%。

（三）主要市場方面，9 月對美國、東協出口值分創歷年單月次高、新高，
年增率各為 1.1 倍及 93.4%，合計對總出口增加貢獻達 7 成；對中國
大陸與香港、歐洲、日本出口各年增 30.2%、20.6%、13.4%。累計 1
至 9 月對五大市場出口值皆為歷年同期最高，其中對美國、東協均
提前刷新全年紀錄 ， 分別年增59.3%、52.9%，對歐洲出口年增48.8%，
對陸港、日本各增 28.5%、26.6%。
（四）展望未來，全球貿易政策及地緣政治情勢變化等，仍增添總體 經濟
不確定性，惟隨人工智慧應用 持續深化，國際雲端服務業者 及各國

市場疑慮拖累美股，短線聚焦 Fed 決策與財報
2026 年 9 月 15 日
一、 9/14 美股下挫、美債殖利率曲線熊市趨平，且油價大漲
1. 股票指數：
⚫ 道瓊工業指數下跌 0.29%，收 52,421.20 點；
⚫ S&P 500 指數下挫 0.48%，收 7,619.98 點；
⚫ 那斯達克綜合指數下跌 0.56%，收 26,186.41 點；
⚫ 費城半導體指數下跌 5.86%，收 11,131.28 點。
2. 當日重要個股表現：
⚫ 在科技類股方面，輝達下跌約 3.4%、美光下跌 5.3%、邁威爾下跌 7.3%；

⚫ 通訊類股方面，Netflix 上漲 3.8%、Take-Two 上漲 3.5%、Fox 上漲 4.6%；
⚫ 軟體類股方面 ，ServiceNow、Adobe、Workday 等軟體股則上漲約 4% 至
7.4%。
3. 美國債市漲跌不一、殖利率曲線走勢熊市趨平。2 年期美國公債殖利率上升 3.24 個基
點至 4.66%，10 年期殖利率上升 2.06 個基點至 4.99%，10 年期殖利率一度升破 5%，
而 30 年期殖利率則下跌約 0.76 個基點至 5.34%。短率上升，反映市場對未來美國聯
準會升息的預期。
4. 油價因中東衝突與供給風險再度升高而上漲 ，WTI 原油上漲 1.81%，收在每桶 101.86
美元，布蘭特原油則上漲 1.56%，收在 106.24 美元。
二、 下跌原因分析
1. AI 開發放緩疑慮，壓抑硬體需求預期：Anthropic 執行長 Dario Amodei 於上週末
呼籲放慢前沿 AI 能力提升速度，並提出第三方安全評估與業界協調方案，使 AI 擴

張節奏成為新的不確定因素。我們研判，半導體位於 AI 建置支出的上游，股價對新
增算力投資的成長速度尤其敏感。即使既有訂單未立即改變，只要市場降低對未來數
季增速的期待，GPU、高頻寬記憶體及網通設備供應鏈的評價便可能先行調整；目前
仍須區分投資預期降溫與實際訂單下修。
2. 中東能源設施遇襲，油價推升通膨風險：沙烏地阿拉伯能源設施及中東船舶遇襲，引
發供應疑慮，布蘭特原油期貨 9 月 14 日結算價升至每桶 106.24 美元。油價持續偏
高可能透過運輸與生產成本以及民眾實質購買力影響企業獲利；若進一步傳導至通膨

預期，將延長貨幣政策維持緊縮的時間，對股市評價形成壓力。
3. 十年美債殖利率一度突破 5%，評價與融資承壓：美國 10 年期公債殖利率盤中突破
5%，為 2023 年以來首見。殖利率上升提高未來獲利的折現率，對依賴長期成長預
期支撐評價的股票較不利。另一方面，資料中心若仰賴借款擴張，融資成本上升也會
提高投資報酬門檻，可能影響新增建置計畫，形成 AI 供應鏈面臨的另一項壓力。
4. 通膨月增回升 ， 升息前觀望氣氛加重 ：BLS 於 9 月 11 日公布 ，8 月 CPI 月增 0.4%
，高於 7 月的 0.1%，核心 CPI 月增率也由 0.2% 升至 0.3%。我們認為，短期物
價動能回升，加上能源價格偏高，提高聯準會維持緊縮政策的壓力。投資人除了評估
當次升息，也須重新衡量後續利率高點與維持時間，增加 FOMC 會議前高波動股票
的調整壓力。
5. 成長與評價雙重重估，放大半導體跌幅：本波股市回檔壓力同時來自獲利成長預期與
市場願給的評價倍數。AI 開發放緩疑慮影響未來營收想像，殖利率上行則壓低未來獲

利的現值。當股價已反映較高成長期待，只要新增訊息不如預期，即使當期營收仍強
勁，也可能出現明顯修正。後續應觀察獲利上修能否抵銷評價收縮，不能僅以跌幅大
就認定股價便宜。

三、市場看法
1. 龍頭財報仍具成長，支持中期正向看法：輝達 8 月 26 日公布的 2027 會計年度第
二季資料中心營收達 890 億美元、年增 117%，並預估第三季總營收 1,080 億美元
、上下浮動 2%。博通 9 月 2 日公布的 2026 會計年度第三季 AI 半導體營收為
167 億美元、年增 221%，並預估第四季達 217 億美元。上述實績與公司展望，是
我們對 AI 核心供應鏈維持中期正向看法的依據；惟這些財測發布於本次事件之前，
仍須追蹤後續更新。
2. 放緩方案尚待落地，需求影響須分項評估：Anthropic 執行長 Amodei 明確表示，放

緩不代表停止模型訓練，但也提出研議訓練算力等限制，因此實際影響取決於後續執
行方式。既有模型的商業使用、推論與安全測試仍可能帶來算力需求，惟能否抵銷前
沿訓練投資的放緩，必須由使用量、訂單與收入驗證。此時適合保留成長觀點，同時
提高對支出變化的敏感度。
3. 科技股仍有輪動，軟體與半導體強弱分明：9/14 當日企業軟體與資安股逆勢上漲，顯
示科技股內部仍有輪動與受惠程度的差異。我們認為，若前沿 AI 開發放緩，部分既
有軟體業者面臨的競爭及產品替代壓力可能減輕；AI 安全疑慮升高，也可能強化資安
、權限控管及企業治理需求。相較之下，半導體更直接承受新增建置支出預期變化。
因此，9/14 當日費半重挫尚不足以推論整體科技需求同步轉弱；後續宜比較訂單能見
度、訂閱收入、獲利與現金流，單日相對強勢仍須由基本面延續性驗證。
4. 升息機率約九成，會後政策態度才是關鍵：依 Reuters 於美東 9 月 14 日收盤報導
引述的 CME FedWatch 資料，本次升息一碼的機率約 90%。若美東 9 月 16 日

FOMC 確實升息 0.25 個百分點，利率區間將升至 3.75% 至 4.00%，當次升息已
大致反映於市場預期。我們認為，會後聲明及主席記者會對未來利率政策的態度才是
關鍵：是否暗示還需升息、利率須維持高檔多久，以及是否擔心能源漲價向核心通膨

擴散，並須搭配點陣圖判讀。若後續緊縮訊號未超出預期、長債殖利率趨穩，有利評
價修復；若利率路徑較預期更高、更持久，科技股仍可能承壓。
5. 通膨改善仍不均衡，避免過早判定轉折：8 月核心 CPI 年增率為 2.4%，低於 7 月
的 2.5%，但其月增率由 0.2% 升至 0.3%，顯示年增放緩與短期動能回升並存。後
續須觀察能源漲價是否向服務價格與通膨預期擴散。若油價回穩、核心物價再度降溫
，利率壓力可望緩和；若能源衝擊持續，股市調整時間可能拉長。
四、投資建議
1. 短線聚焦 Fed 決策與 10 月美股財報，科技股中期向上趨勢料不致有變：我們目前仍

維持 AI 核心供應鏈中期正向看法，較看好能將營收成長轉化為獲利與現金流的企業
，但宜密切觀察本週的 FOMC 會議結果與 10 月中將起跑的美股財報。若雲端業者維
持資本支出、晶片公司財測穩定，且 FOMC 此次會議未釋出超預期緊縮訊號，有利
市場信心修復；然而，若出現廣泛支出下修、訂單遞延，或油價與殖利率持續上行，
則應拉長觀察期並重新檢視通膨與利率對於股市動能前景的影響。
2. 3~10 年美國公債/投資級債為債市首選：目前美國的通膨數據仍具韌性，且就業市場
穩健，使年底前市場升息預期再度升溫。市場對美國財政赤字、公債供給及長期通膨
仍要求較高風險補償，30 年期殖利率與短天期利差仍維持高檔，顯示期限溢酬尚未收
斂，考量當前 3～10 年期美國公債與投資級債能鎖定目前較高息收，且其受長端財政
風險影響也相對較小，故現階段3～10 年期美國公債及投資級債的風險報酬結構相對
較佳。

## Document 253: Capital Market Outlook (2026-10-05)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Capital Market Outlook (2026-10-05).pdf
Report source: Merrill Lynch (CMO)
Date: 2026-10-05
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

CHIEF INVESTMENT OFFICE
Capital Market Outlook

### Page 2

2 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Stronger Growth, Costlier Money
Chief Investment Office, Macro Strategy Team
The latest GDP report and annual revisions show a sturdier U.S. economy than previously
reported. According to the Bureau of Economic Analysis (BEA), real GDP grew at a 2.2%
annualized pace in Q2 compared to 1.5% previously estimated, and Q1 was revised
from 2.1% to 2.5%. Both 2024 and 2025 were also revised slightly higher. Most of the

first half of the year revision was in categories related to the Artificial Intelligence (AI)
buildout, but real consumer spending has also been revised slightly higher, accelerating
from 0.7% annualized growth in Q1 to 3.8% in Q2 despite surging energy prices and
slow hiring.
Spending in July and August remained much stronger than labor market conditions alone
would suggest, also averaging 3.7% annualized growth. Upside revisions to net interest

income and personal saving suggest households have benefited from more income than
was previously apparent, with personal saving revised higher back to 2023. The new data,
according to the BEA, show the January to July 2026 savings rate averaged 4.8% versus
3.3% prerevision, strengthening consumer spending underpinnings despite moderate
hiring, high energy prices and rising borrowing costs.
The BEA update also included inflation methodological changes related to categories

inflated by the AI boom—such as portfolio management fees and computer software and
accessories. These improved the inflation picture to some extent—August “core” PCE
inflation was reduced by 0.3 ppts to 3% YoY, bringing it closer to “core” consumer price
index inflation, which was 2.5% YoY and 2% on a three-month annualized basis. While still
above the 2% Federal Reserve (Fed) target, “core” PCE now appears stable at 3% year over
year since January rather than accelerating. These methodological changes are expected

to dampen pass-through of AI-related price pressures into “core” inflation and effects of
stock-market swings on inflation.
They also produced an encouraging improvement in recent PCE inflation momentum.
Three-month annualized “core” PCE inflation slowed from 4.3% at the peak of tariff
effects in February to 2.1% in August. For durable goods, this rate was down from 5.8%
in February to 1.5% in August, and, for services ex-energy and healthcare, it slowed from

4.2% to 2.2%, for example. Inflation is volatile on a three-month change basis, so it
remains to be seen whether this moderation continues without further Fed tightening.
For now, the trends in the San Francisco Fed’s cyclical versus acyclical inflation measures
diverged again in August. As expected, given softening wage growth, prices most sensitive
to the labor market have eased further. At 3% YoY, this inflation category is down from
8% in early 2023 and 4.6% two years ago is back to prepandemic levels and is likely

to ease more (Exhibit 1A). In contrast, supply bottlenecks, high energy prices and other
industry-specific factors have kept acyclical inflation on an uptrend. As shown in Exhibit
1B, the relentless increase in freight costs could prolong upside pressure on this category.
The diesel market has tightened in recent months, boosting its price by 70% YoY,
consistent with Brent oil at around $200 per barrel and large reverberations across
a wide swath of goods and services prices. A government-policy-related decline in

available truck drivers is also raising transportation costs more than expected. So are
tight ocean shipping capacity and longer/more expensive voyages due to the U.S./Iran
War. Supply-chain stress also increased as companies rushed to front-run potential new
tariff increases.
On the positive side for the inflation outlook, unit labor costs (ULC) are up modestly
as robust productivity growth has absorbed much of the wage growth, limiting a major
source of domestic inflation. Upside productivity growth revisions are likely to further

reduce ULC, which could help shift focus from energy and AI-related inflation to
eventual disinflationary effects once oil and refined fuel prices start to ease. Sustained
productivity gains would support growth and margins while limiting inflation and the
need for much higher interest rates.
Investment Implications
We favor selective Equity exposure
to companies with durable demand
and pricing power, while remaining
cautious about businesses most

exposed to fuel, freight, and
financing costs. We would also
gradually increase long-duration
exposure.

### Page 3

3 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
Surging nominal growth has boosted corporate revenues (up 13% YoY in Q2, according to the
BEA) while contained unit labor costs helped raise margins and profits more than expected.
With labor costs declining as a share of revenues, investment-related tax incentives and
rock-bottom net interest expense, pre-tax nonfinancial corporate profits as measured in GDP
accounts rose 21% YoY in Q2. Total corporate profits after-tax were up 18.2% YoY, according to

the BEA.
Surging AI-related imports and investment are keeping the business and profits cycle on
an uptrend both here and abroad. According to BofA Global Research, the global economic
cycle backdrop improved further in September, as reflected in a broad-based increase in its
Global Wave1 indicator. Broadening U.S. manufacturing strength, fueled by ongoing AI-driven
investment, a healthier orders backlog and low inventories suggest that the expansion has

room to run—a constructive environment for earnings. Typically, a Global Wave upswing is
consistent with rising earnings revisions ratios and equity price uptrends. According to the
Global Wave, global earnings growth expectations remain exceptionally strong.
Still, absent a resolution of war-related energy and transportation disruptions, the same
industrial strength intensifies inflation pressures. A prolonged supply shock raises the risk of
restrictive interest rates and weakening economic growth. In this context, the surge in the

10-year Treasury yield above 5% is starting to reflect more than optimism about U.S. growth
and strengthening productivity outlook. Oil’s tightest recent correlation with 10-year Treasury
yields in 35 years suggests a common energy-price thread behind rising yields in developed
countries.
Bond market spillovers from normalizing Japan yields and capital repatriation are another
common thread. Higher yields on foreign sovereign bonds such as in Japan—which has

escaped stagnation and deflation by intensely pursuing investment-led growth—weaken the
relative appeal of Treasurys. That distinction matters for portfolios. A yield increase driven
mainly by durable productivity gains would come with a stronger outlook for future income
and earnings. A yield increase driven by cost shocks and greater uncertainty raises financing
costs without the same benefit.
In sum, the economy remains more resilient than expected, but the bond market is also

charging more for an uncertain inflation outlook given untethered government debt and
cooling global demand for Treasurys spurred by geopolitical tensions and Japan capital
repatriation. The case for much further tightening becomes stronger if wage growth
strengthens, productivity lags, and unit labor costs reaccelerate, causing cyclical inflation to
also pick up steam. The case becomes weaker if that measure stays contained while fuel and
freight pressures recede or rising Treasury yields reduce asset prices and economic growth.

4 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
Yields, Yen and Iran: What Haunts the “House”
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
We recently highlighted the three-front challenge facing Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent:
engineering a stronger yen, containing the 10-year Treasury yield and securing a diplomatic
resolution to the conflict with Iran.

Bessent recently has delivered one of the year’s more memorable lines while discussing
Treasury policy and the yen: “I have asymmetric information. I am the house now.” 2 Yet several
challenges remain: the 10-year Treasury yield is near levels last seen in the early 2000s, the
yen remains structurally weaker, and a phased agreement to reopen the Strait of Hormuz
appears remote. The resulting strain on energy markets keeps inflation risks elevated.

Put differently, oil prices, Treasury yields and the dollar-yen exchange rate are all trending
against Bessent’s objectives. Below, we examine how each variable is haunting his “house.”
Haunting the “House” is the Yen. Since the U.S. and Japan mounted a record $98.7B
coordinated intervention in late July after the yen weakened to a 40-year low near 164 per
dollar, the currency has appreciated roughly 4% against the dollar. The move was further
supported on September 18 when the BoJ raised rates by 25 basis points (bps) to 1.25%,

their highest level since the mid-1990s (Exhibit 2A). However, the split 7–2 vote and Governor
Ueda’s limited forward guidance leave the path of further tightening uncertain. The key
question is whether additional BoJ hikes can produce sustained appreciation in the yen, or
merely reinforce the temporary relief provided by the summer intervention.
Bessent, meanwhile, has repeatedly signaled his preference for a stronger yen. Partly because
yen stability is also a U.S. financing concern. Persistent currency weakness could prompt

Japan to support its currency by selling foreign assets, including U.S. Treasurys—which would
be an unwelcome prospect as Washington partially relies on foreign capital flows to fund its
large fiscal deficits. That potential “haunts” the Treasury Secretary.  At precisely the moment
Washington needs foreign capital to finance its enormous fiscal deficits, it would be inopportune
for one of America’s largest creditors to become a significant seller of Treasury securities.

Exhibit 2: Charting the “Yentervention” and U.S. Treasury Yields Surge Across the Curve.
A) The Yen Regains Some Strength. B) 5-Year Forward Yields Hold Above 5%.

Haunting the “House” are Yields. The surge in yields runs counter to a White House intent
on lowering borrowing costs. The benchmark 10-year yield has broken above the 5% threshold
and climbed to levels not seen since 2007. At around 5.30%, it stands more than a full
percentage point above where it began the year (Exhibit 2B). The level is striking but so is the
speed of the move: Stronger-than-expected economic data, including a survey showing U.S.

2 September 8, 2026.
Investment Implications

complicating the macro backdrop
(i.e., higher-for-longer yields/oil)
the combination of strong earnings
growth, robust consumer demand
(real final sales to private domestic
purchasers grew at 4.6% for Q2) and
ongoing capital investment suggests
that the fundamental backdrop for
U.S. Equities remains constructive.

### Page 5

5 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
business activity expanded at the fastest pace in more than five years, helped trigger an 18
bps surge in the 10-year yield—among its largest daily moves in decades. The 5-year yield also
topped 5% for the first time since 2007, while the 30-year yield reached 5.63%, its highest
level since 2002.
Compounding the move is inflation, running above the Fed’s 2% target for 67 months,

large fiscal deficits and the prospect of continued growth in Treasury issuance. Against this
backdrop, Treasury bought back $4.1 billion of long-dated debt, versus a maximum of $6
billion. That effort has done little to arrest the selloff. A subsequent auction priced nearly $4
billion of 7-year notes at 5.085%, the highest yield for that maturity in 33 years.
All in, the MOVE index, a key gauge of Treasury market volatility, jumped from 78 to 95 on

Wednesday September 23 and then to 104 the following day, its highest level since the early
days of the Iran war (Exhibit 3A).
Haunting the “House” is the Iran conflict. Early expectations that military pressure and
economic sanctions would quickly bring Iran to the negotiating table have yet to materialize.
Iranian officials have signaled that a full reopening of the Strait of Hormuz remains unlikely
while sanctions stay in place. Although talks between Washington and Tehran are continuing,

conflicting signals and lingering skepticism from both sides have kept tensions elevated.
As a result, oil prices have remained elevated in a range even as some Middle East supply
disruptions began to ease.
Startling is that Brent crude is up more than 60% this year, but refined product prices
have risen even faster. Ongoing disruptions to energy flows through the Strait of Hormuz,
combined with Ukrainian strikes on Russian refining infrastructure have tightened global

supplies of crude oil and refined fuels. Saudi Arabia has restored some drone-disrupted flows,
providing roughly 3.5 million barrels a day of relief (roughly 3% of global consumption).
The squeeze is mostly acute in diesel markets, where inventories remain constrained and
prices continue to climb (Exhibit 3B). U.S. diesel prices recently surpassed $6.50 per gallon,
the highest level on record, just as Russia has extended restrictions on diesel exports into

October. Against this backdrop, proposals to ban U.S. fuel exports risks worsening the
imbalance. According to S&P Global, an export ban could reduce refinery runs by nearly 2
million barrels per day and significantly cut gasoline production, potentially driving gasoline
and jet fuel prices even higher. As long as tensions persist, the oil impulse remains a key
impediment to structurally lower inflation.
Investment implications. Treasury Secretary Bessent increasingly sits at the intersection of

monetary policy, fiscal policy, foreign policy and national security. We remain constructive on
the U.S. economy and overweight U.S. Equities but are watching as these fronts challenge the
narrative. There is a great deal riding on the “house” versus the key macro variables of today.

### Page 6

6 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
When It Comes to Attracting Foreign Capital, the U.S. Remains Exceptional
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
There may be no better vote of confidence in a country than the willingness of
foreigners to put their money there. And by that measurement, the U.S. remains the
exception—both portfolio and FDI inflows are currently running at record highs based

on the latest data. This dynamic is one key reason why we remain constructive on U.S.
assets over the longer term.
Notwithstanding all the handwringing about America’s fiscal deficits, tariffs and rising
government debt, global investors sunk some $942B in net purchases of U.S. Equities in
the 12 months to July, the highest rolling 12-month total on record. In Q2, net purchase
of U.S. Equities soared to $426B, up 62% from the same period a year ago (Exhibit 4A).

By country, flows were down from China—not surprisingly—but strong from Taiwan and
South Korea, who have taken to recycling their massive AI-generated trade surplus back
into the U.S. In the end, strong portfolio inflows suggest that international investors
remain willing to increase their exposure to the world's largest capital market—that’s
bullish for U.S. assets.
Meanwhile, Exhibit 4B highlights the growing push among foreign multinationals to be

inside the U.S., with FDI inflows totaling nearly $240B in the first six months of this year.
That is a staggering 103% jump from a year ago and means FDI inflows are on track for
a record year, estimated at $480B for 2026.
There are multiple factors behind surging inflows: One, due to rising U.S. trade
protectionism, foreign multinationals increasingly want to be “inside” the U.S. economy,
not outside. Two, within the $32 trillion-plus economy with extraordinarily consumer

purchasing power, foreign firms prefer to be closer to their customers via investment
than just trade (exports). Three, America’s massive investment cycle in AI, data centers,
semiconductors, power generation, etc., has created numerous potential investment
opportunities for many foreign firms that don’t want to miss out on America’s surging
capex cycle. Four, and finally, abundant energy supplies, deep capital markets and a highly
developed technological ecosystem have also induced more FDI inflows.

In the end, double-barreled capital inflows speak to the absolute and relative
attractiveness of the U.S. economy and capital markets. Stay invested in America—the
rest of the world is.
Exhibit 4: U.S. Exceptionalism: Record Inflows of Portfolio and FDI Inflows.
A) Net Foreign Purchases of US Equities and Investment Funds. B) FDI Inflows to the U.S.

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

ICE BofA MOVE Index is a market-implied measure of U.S. bond market volatility.
Consumer Price Index measures the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a market basket of consumer goods and services.
Baltic Dry Index is a daily shipping and trade index published by the Baltic Exchange in London that measures the cost of moving raw materials like iron ore, coal, and grain by sea.
Volatility Index (VIX) is a real-time market indicator that measures the stock market's expected volatility over the next 30 days.

This material does not take into account a client’s particular investment objectives, financial situations, or needs and is not intended as a recommendation, offer, or solicitation for the purchase
or sale of any security or investment strategy. Merrill offers a broad range of brokerage, investment advisory and other services. There are important differences between brokerage and

of America, Merrill or any affiliate to sell or provide, or a specific invitation for a consumer to apply for, any particular retail financial product or service that may be available.

oversight and data analytics. CIO viewpoints are developed for Bank of America Private Bank, a division of Bank of America, N.A., (“Bank of America”) and Merrill Lynch, Pierce, Fenner & Smith

The Global Wealth & Investment Management Investment Strategy Committee (“GWIM ISC”) is responsible for developing and coordinating recommendations for short-term and long-term
investment strategy and market views encompassing markets, economic indicators, asset classes and other market-related projections affecting GWIM.

Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

## Document 293: First Impressions_ NZ household incomes, assets and savings update - June quarter 2026 (2026-10-22)

### Page 1

#consumer #newzealand
First Impressions: NZ household incomes, assets and savings
update - June quarter 2026
By Satish Ranchhod
Senior Economist, Westpac NZ
10:24 October 08 2026 Share
New Zealand incomes and savings are rising, as is the value of households’ financial
assets. However, the value of housing assets remains flat.
Key takeouts from Stats NZ’s latest update on household incomes, assets and savings (June quarter
2026)

### Page 2

Household incomes
Disposable income levels in the economy grew by 5.2% in the year to June, down from 6.5% in the
year to March

Savings and wealth
Household savings have continued to rise, climbing by $2.5b over the past quarter.

Overall household wealth levels were up 1.9% over the past year. Under the surface, the make-up of
household wealth continues to shift.

Interest costs 
Overall household income levels are continuing to rise. Much of that is due to increases in
entrepreneurial earnings (which is in part due to increased earnings in the agricultural sector). Wage
and salary growth has been more limited but has picked up.
Household savings levels have been rising for three years now, with another solid rise over the past
three months. 
Overall household wealth levels have risen modestly. Although the value of households’ financial
assets is continuing to rise, housing assets have essentially shown no growth since late 2023.
Household spending on interest costs rose just slightly in the June quarter. That’s the first increase in
two years. The easing in average borrowing costs seen over the past few years has come to a close
with the RBNZ’s hiking cycle now in train.
Underpinning the rise in overall income levels have been continued increases in entrepreneurial
earnings, which are up 12% over the past year. That will include stronger earnings in the agricultural
sector, which has been boosted by the strength in commodity export prices. That lift in agricultural
earnings has been reflected in stronger economic confidence and spending in many rural regions.
We expect commodity export earnings will remain firm over the year ahead. 
In contrast, wage and salary earnings are up just 2.7% over the past year. That’s up from 1.8% in the
year to March, but it’s a fairly muted rise, especially given the continued increases in the population
and inflation of 4.1%.
June’s large increase in savings builds on the solid increases seen over the past three years.
The savings rate in the June quarter was 3.7% of disposable incomes, down just slightly from last
quarter.
The value of households’ financial assets has continued to rise and is up 4.6% over the past year. 
However, the value of housing and land assets is down 0.9% over the past year, and it has remained
essentially unchanged since late 2023. We expect housing market conditions will remain soft over
the coming year, limiting increases in overall household wealth. 
Households’ spending on interest costs rose just slightly in the June quarter, climbing by 0.3%.
While that’s a relatively small change, it is the first increase that we’ve seen in two years.

### Page 3

Related articles

Media Contact:
Satish Ranchhod, Senior Economist
P: +64 9 336 5668
M: +64 21 710 852
Over the past few years, many borrowers saw their debt servicing costs fall as they rolled off earlier
high mortgage rates and onto lower ones. That helped to support household spending.
However, the easing in average borrowing cost has now ended. Recent months have seen mortgage
rates pushing higher as the RBNZ has begun to hike the Official Cash Rate, with further hikes
expected over the coming year. 
Looking ahead, we don’t expect a significant increase in average household borrowing costs.
However, we won’t see the same support for spending that we did over the past few years.

## Document 308: Global Strategy Views_ Competition for Capital

Source PDF: all report pdf/Global Strategy Views_ Competition for Capital.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs Publishing
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 12
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1. There are two themes dominating our investor conversations: the impact of AI and 
the rise in interest rates. The two issues are linked as demand for capital from both 
the private and public sectors increase. In the private sector a surge in capex 
spending to fund AI infrastructure has eaten into free cash flow and forced 
companies to raise more in debt and equity markets. Meanwhile, government 
borrowing needs have increased as priorities shift towards upgrading critical 
infrastructure, energy security and defense at a time when cyclical inflationary 
pressures driven by higher energy prices are also resulting in higher policy rates. The 
combination has pushed up the cost of capital. As recently as 2022, for example, 
30-year bond yields in Germany and Japan were close to zero (Exhibit 1). A rise in 
yields, together with more uncertainty (over geopolitics and the future impact of AI) 
have, in combination, pushed up the cost of capital.

2. This rise in yields comes after a near-record period of equity outperformance 
relative to bonds over 10-year holding periods (Exhibit 2).

Exhibit 1: Rising yields and uncertainty have increased the cost of capital 
30-year government bond benchmark yields (%)

As equities have continued to outperform bonds, equity risk premia have fallen back to 
levels last seen in the late 1990s, leaving equity markets more vulnerable to further 
increases in bond yields. That said, in the US and Japan, the ERP has bounced off recent 
lows (Exhibit 3).

3. The impact of rising bond yields, however, depends not only on the level of yields, but 
also on the reasons as well as the speed of the adjustment (Exhibit 4). It is the speed 
issue that has caused the biggest impact in the recent past. As our US strategists note, 
stocks have usually generated positive returns alongside rising interest rates unless the 
pace of rising rates exceeded 2+ standard deviations and this is a pattern that is broadly

4. But despite the rise in yields, nominal GDP and profit growth remain strong and, until 
recently, the impact of rising bond yields has been offset by the strength in corporate 
profits. Earnings growth has been the main driver of equity returns over the past 18 
months in all regions (Exhibit 5). This strength has meant that PE multiples have either 
been flat (Japan and Europe) or have fallen (the US, Asia and EM).

### Page 4

the start of the year to 19x, in line with its long-run average, despite the market being 
close to its all-time high. 
5. While US equities have driven the bulk of equity outperformance for much of the 
fifteen years post the financial crisis, the improvement in earnings across regions, and 
the sharper de-rating in the US, has resulted in equity performance being 
broader-based by geography. Since 2025, the US equity market has been the weakest of 
the major regions (Exhibit 6), a turnaround from the dominant trend since the financial 
crisis and increasing investor returns from a more geographically diversified portfolio.

6. Supportive profit growth has been accompanied by positive earnings revisions, with 
2026 and 2027 estimates moving higher across major regions. There are four broad 
areas which have been driving much of this profit growth. First, technology earnings 
continue to be very robust. Second, rising energy prices have pushed up profits in the 
commodity sector. Third, banks have generally enjoyed strong earnings backed by 
positive nominal GDP growth, steep yield curves and strong private sector balance 
sheets. Finally, sectors such as Industrials have benefited from the surge in AI capex 
spending which has spilled over into improved revenues for the ‘pick and shovels’ of AI 
infrastructure. The breadth of the earnings’ growth has also increased the opportunity 
for investors to diversify across sectors as well as countries.

### Page 5

7. While technology profit growth has remained strong, the surge in capex spending 
among the hyperscalers has increasingly eaten through their free cash flow (Exhibit 8) 
prompting companies to look for alternative sources of funding, in the credit and equity 
markets.  The capex spending growth for AA-rated issuers has been substantial: the 65% 
year-over-year growth in Q2 marks the 10th consecutive quarter that aggregate AA 
capex growth exceeds 35%. They have also turned to the convertible bond market 
where volume has also increased year to date reaching $135 billion in the US, with 
AI-related borrowers driving 44% of total issuance, on our estimates. 
Our Credit team raised their full-year US IG gross issuance forecast by $200bn to $2.3 
trillion and AI-related issuers account for a quarter of USD IG gross supply this year, 
pushing overall supply to a year-to-date record.

The combination of higher cost of capital and greater capital intensity in the tech sector 
has reduced the value of their future cash flows and triggered a de-rating in the biggest 
companies.  The dominant capex hyperscalers in the US now have a forward PE close to 
the average of the rest of the market.

8. Software companies have also de-rated on the back of fears of disruption from AI 
while memory and chips stocks have also experienced lower multiples as their earnings

### Page 7

surge. Globally technology has seen its PE ratio moderate, falling below its 20-year 
median (Exhibit 10). Meanwhile, the spending of these companies has spilled over into 
better earnings and more visible cash flows in many other parts of the economy. 
Healthcare, Consumer Discretionary and Industrials are all more expensive in absolute 
terms than IT, and Industrials has re-rated and now has a PE close to the 90th percentile 
of its 20-year range.

9. For these reasons, we have argued that Technology equities have not experienced a 
classic bubble because valuation levels are much lower than in previous financial 
bubbles. Nevertheless, the risk for investors is less about valuation and more whether 
there is an unsustainable earnings bubble. Bubbles in earnings, rather than valuations, 
have occurred in previous periods.  The Bank sector, for example, briefly became the 
biggest sector in the S&P 500 in the run up to the financial crisis of 2008/09 (Exhibit 11). 
Unlike the technology bubble of the late 1990s, or the Japanese bubble of the late 
1980s, Bank stocks did not experience a major valuation bubble at the time, but the 
surge in earnings that drove the sector’s outperformance proved to be unsustainable.

### Page 8

There are, however, important differences to the Tech sector today. The earnings of the 
banks were boosted by sharply rising leverage and the asset that they were financing – 
US real estate – did experience a valuation bubble. When the housing market collapsed, 
pushing economies into recession, it triggered a sharp fall in underlying earnings for the 
banking sector. There are important differences today. First, technology profits remain 
very robust, and balance sheets are strong overall. Second, interest coverage ratios for 
the aggregate S&P 500 and its median stock, for example, rank in the 99th and 68th 
percentiles relative to the past 20 years. Third, demand for compute is accelerating and 
outstripping supply; Microsoft recently declared their intent to triple data centre 
capacity in 6 years. The bottlenecks are increasingly power, land and data centre 
capacity rather than a lack of compute demand. At our annual Communacopia 
Technology Conference, Nvidia reiterated its 2030 AI TAM outlook to $3tn-$4tn and 
supply constraints continue to be broad based. Nonetheless, any slowdown in profit 
growth, in an environment of a much higher cost of capital, could put downward 
pressure on equity prices, reducing confidence in future cash flows across the ecosystem 
from the hyperscalers to the ‘pick and shovels’ that have been benefiting from the capex 
boom.  
10. Given the near-term risks in bond markets, we remain neutral on equities, alongside 
other asset classes on a 3-month horizon, but continue to be overweight equities over 
12 months. While profit growth is likely to slow from the recent strong pace, ongoing 
growth in nominal GDP should underpin equity markets and the earnings cycle 
propelling equity returns even without any valuation expansion from current levels and 
our economists continue to see a low risk of recession. As our asset allocation team has 
shown, earnings growth and ROE are likely to continue to be the key drivers of equity 
performance relative to bonds, and we expect these fundamentals to remain supportive 
for equity returns. Nevertheless, we continue to recommend well diversified equity 
exposure as opportunities continue to broaden across geographies, sectors and factors.

### Page 9

Stock correlations are at multi-year lows across the major equity markets (Exhibit 12).

Even within sectors like technology, correlations have fallen sharply (Exhibit 13). The 
‘magnificent seven’ that was long seen as a homogeneous group of technology winners 
has also seen significant dispersion of returns. Differentiation, diversification and alpha 
generation are likely to remain the key drivers of equity returns. With higher bond yields, 
valuations are not likely to rise making earnings the critical driver of returns for investors.

## Document 326: Kicking and screaming_ Child care costs persist Child care remains a constraint for many families’ budgets, with the average share of monthly child care 8% of a household’s pay. 2 

Source PDF: all report pdf/Kicking and screaming_ Child care costs persist Child care remains a constraint for many families’ budgets, with the average share of monthly child care 8% of a household’s pay. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 7
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

13030323     1

Economy 
Kicking and screaming: Child care costs persist 
20 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Child care remains a growing financial burden for many households, even as growth in the median payment has moderated, 
according to Bank of America payments data. More families are paying more than $2,000 per month for care, suggesting 
affordability pressures are becoming increasingly concentrated among households facing the highest expenses. 
• Child care cost pressures vary significantly across the country. Nashville and Jacksonville, FL posted the fastest year-over-year 
growth among major metro areas, while New York and Washington DC saw the biggest decrease in median payment growth, 
according to Bank of America payments data. 
• Limited supply may continue to constrain access to child care for some households. Child care employment fell 0.7% year-over-
year in September, suggesting provider capacity remains under pressure and could continue to affect household finances and 
labor force participation.

Child care costs outpace rent and medical services 
Child care remains a significant expense for many families. In September, the growth in child care and other school-related fees 
surpassed other major household expenses such as rent and medical services, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) 
(Exhibit 1). 
Yet, while child care prices continue to rise in official inflation data, median household payments have largely moderated since 
February, according to Bank of America data (Exhibit 2). More importantly, the growth in the number of households paying for 
child care has fallen since the start of 2025. 
Exhibit 1: The cost of child care has surpassed other services such as 
rent in the consumer price index  
Consumer price index by category (monthly, seasonally adjusted, YoY%)

Source: Haver Analytics  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

1.0% year-over-year (YoY) in September but the number of 
households paying for child care fell 1.0% YoY

Fewer lower-income households are paying for child care 
Why is Bank of America payment growth trending in the opposite direction of the Consumer Price Index? Households are likely 
changing the amount of care they use, which is reflected in slower growth in the number paying for child care. As more

households shift into lower-cost care (school-age, public pre-K, subsidized or free after-school programs), median payments can 
fall even as prices continue rising.  
The decline in the number of households paying for child care may also reflect families seeking alternatives to formal care 
arrangements. Some may be relying more heavily on relatives, shifting parents’ work schedules or dropping out of the workforce 
altogether to care for their children in response to elevated costs. This could explain why, among all income groups paying for 
care, the number of lower-income households has seen the biggest decline, falling 2.0% year-over-year (YoY) in September 
(Exhibit 3). 
Exhibit 3: The number of lower-income households paying for child care has fallen the most, down 2.0% YoY in September 
Number of households with child care payments (monthly, three-month moving average, YoY%)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
More families face larger monthly bills 
Yet even as fewer households pay for child care, the share of those shelling out more than $2,000 per month has increased 
substantially (Exhibit 4). In fact, it has more than doubled since 2023, reaching 7.7% in 2026 year-to-date. Meanwhile, the share 
of those paying $1,000 or less has declined, suggesting higher monthly payments are becoming more common even as the 
national median grows more slowly. 
Exhibit 4: The share of households paying >$2000 a month for child care has more than doubled since 2023 
Monthly child care payments by amount (annual average, share of households, %)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Rising costs take a bigger bite out of households’ paychecks 
What makes child care “affordable”? According to the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services, child care is considered 
affordable if it accounts for no more than 7% of family income. Yet, in Bank of America data, the average child care cost was 8% 
in September.  
While an 8% share may not appear dramatically above the federal affordability benchmark of 7%, it represents an average across 
households’ income levels. For families in high-cost metro areas or those paying more than $2,000 per month, the burden is 
likely considerably higher.

Notably, lower- and middle-income households see outsized shares of their pay dedicated to child care in Bank of America 
payments data, though this has moderated slightly from 2025 (Exhibit 5).  
Exhibit 5: In September, the average child care payment was 8.6% of lower- and middle-income total households’ pay, whereas for higher-income 
families it accounted for 7.3%

BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Where child care costs are climbing 
Child care pressures also differ substantially by location. In Bank of America payments data, Nashville posted the fastest YoY 
median payment growth in September, followed by Jacksonville, FL and Phoenix (Exhibit 6). This dispersion of growth across the 
country suggests child care cost pressures are no longer confined to historically expensive coastal markets.  
Interestingly, New York and Washington DC saw the sharpest decline in growth this year. Why might this be? The supply side of 
the market may help explain why – for example, New York City recently introduced universal Pre-K.1  
Exhibit 6: Nashville and Jacksonville, FL saw the greatest increase in median child care payment growth among select cities in September 
Median child care payments by core-based statistical area in September (YoY%)

Source: Bank of America internal data

Fewer providers, higher costs 
As of September 2026, employment in child care services was down 0.7% from a year earlier, suggesting the sector’s workforce 
recovery has stalled. Fewer workers can constrain the number of available places or the hours providers offer. That may leave 
families facing longer searches, fewer workable arrangements or continued price pressure. In turn, access to care can influence 
parents’ ability to enter, remain in or expand their participation in the workforce. 
Exhibit 7: Employment within child care services fell 0.7% YoY in September 
Employees by sector (monthly, seasonally adjusted, YoY%)

Source: Haver Analytics  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
What are the economic implications of rising child care costs? 
Child care costs may have important labor market implications for families with young children. Of those households paying for 
child care in 2026, more than half of them were Older Millennials (52%), followed by Younger Millennials (20%) and Gen X (16%), 
according to Bank of America payments data. This means child care costs weigh more heavily on Older Millennials. 
The share of Older Millennial households receiving more than one paycheck has continued to grow from a year earlier, but the 
pace of that improvement has moderated over the past three months (Exhibit 8). Given that this group accounts for the majority 
of households paying for child care in Bank of America payments data, they may offer early signs of how affordability affects 
their employment decisions. 
Exhibit 8: The share of Older Millennial households paying for child care that have more than one person with a paycheck has declined since 2021  
Share of households with a child care payment with more than one person with a paycheck (%, monthly, three-month moving average)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Child care challenges can weigh on women’s employment 
According to data from the American Time Use Survey, women continue to perform significantly more child care and household 
work than men, even as their participation in paid work has increased. This suggests that persistent gaps in access and 
affordability may have disproportionate consequences for women’s employment. Indeed, the percentage of women who cited 
child care as a reason for temporary unemployment was nearly three times higher than men’s in 2026, though the average fell 
from 2025, according to data from the BLS. 
Yet labor force participation data indicates a more nuanced picture. Despite persistent child care pressures, mothers are 
participating in the workforce at rates that remain above their 2019 averages.

Mothers’ workforce participation remains elevated, but recent gains have slowed  
However, recent momentum has slowed. While mothers of young children experienced particularly large participation gains in 
2022 and 2023, those have partially reversed in 2024 and 2025 (Exhibit 10).  
In contrast, participation among women with children ages 6 to 17 has continued to edge higher. This signals that the post-
pandemic increase in working has largely been sustained in this group, but the pace of improvement has moderated, especially 
for women caring for younger children.

Methodology 
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Bank of America credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card holders 
making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards is excluded. Data regarding 
merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial services 
companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System (NAICS), 
which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
If applicable, the consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level.  
If applicable, any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a 
deposit account or credit card. Any reference to aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-
peer, cash and checks.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

### Page 2

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
The six-month annualised growth rate in the Westpac–
Melbourne Institute Leading Index, which indicates the
likely pace of economic activity relative to trend three to
nine months into the future, held at –0.03% in September,
unchanged from August.

The Leading Index continues to show moderately improved
momentum compared to the middle of the year, with the
latest reads pointing to growth holding at or slightly below
trend heading into late 2026/early 2027. Growth appears
to be stabilising at a slow pace rather than stalling. That
said, both the latest fuel prices and the RBA’s September
interest rate increase are still working their way through to
the economy with impacts likely to take some of the gloss off

growth near term. Overall, Westpac expects annual growth
to come in at 1.5% for calendar 2026, a below-trend result.
The Leading Index growth rate has firmed slightly over the
last six months, rising from –0.16% back in March to –0.03%
in September. The component detail shows mixed moves over
the period. Four components have contributed more positively
with reduced drags from the Westpac-Melbourne Institute
Unemployment Expectations Index (adding +0.23ppts to

the Index growth rate); the Westpac-Melbourne Institute
Consumer Expectations Index (adding another +0.15ppts);
and the S&P/ASX200 (+0.15ppts) combining with a modest
lift in growth in US industrial production (adding +0.12ppts).
These positives have been partially offset by modest drags
from four previously stable components: dwelling approvals
(–0.34ppts); monthly hours worked (–0.08ppts); commodity
prices, measured in AUD terms (–0.07ppts) and the yield

spread (–0.05ppts).
Overall, the component mix speaks to a lack of direction.
Indeed, despite some large shocks over the last year, the
Leading Index growth rate has been relatively contained since
early 2025, holding in a narrow –0.3% to +0.5% range. This
compares to history which, outside of the COVID and GFC
shocks, has seen growth rates move between a much wider
–1.9% to +1.6% range. Of course, the apparent stability,
which is evident across all components of the index, may not

last but for now it suggests the economy has been stuck in a
bit of a holding pattern over the last two years.
Leading Index still tracking a touch below trend
The Westpac-Melbourne Institute Leading Index of
Economic Activity is designed to assist in identifying
turning points in the economy. The index combines
variables that reflect different aspects of the economy
into a single index that generally produces a more reliable
cyclical indicator than any single component taken

individually. The Leading Index of Economic Activity
provides advance information on the state of the economy
and gives early warnings of cyclical turning points.
Matthew Hassan
Head of Australian Macro-Forecasting

the monthly Westpac-Melbourne Institute Leading Index. We will
continue to publish regular bulletin updates through our westpaciq.
com.au website.

## Document 332: Meta Q3营收与Q4指引双超预期，上调AI资本开支下限，拟发行无投票权新股｜财报见闻

Source PDF: all report pdf/Meta Q3营收与Q4指引双超预期，上调AI资本开支下限，拟发行无投票权新股｜财报见闻.pdf

### Page 1

Meta Q3 营收 646 亿美元，同⽐增⻓ 26% ，预计 Q4 营收 730 亿⾄ 760 亿美元；⾃由现⾦流负
22 亿美元，⾃上市以来⾸次转负，但好于市场预期的负 94 亿美元。公司将 2026 年资本开⽀
指引下限从此前的 1300 亿美元上调⾄ 1350 亿美元，同时宣布创设新的⽆投票权 C 类普通
股，换取扎克伯格承诺留居加州。 Meta 盘后⼀度上涨 6% 。
摘要：
Meta 第三季度营收创历史新⾼，同时宣布将发⾏⽆投票权新类别股票，避免扎克伯格因加州财
富税离开公司总部所在辖区，同时为规模庞⼤的⼈⼯智能投资计划开辟融资渠道。
Meta 周三公布，第三季度营收达 646 亿美元，同⽐增⻓ 26% ，超出分析师预期的 633 亿美元。公
司预计第四季度营收将达到 730 亿⾄ 760 亿美元，区间中值⾼于市场预期。
利润表现则受到⼀次性法律和解费⽤的明显影响。 Meta 第三季度摊薄后每股收益为 3.83 美元，
低于分析师预期的约 6.54 美元。公司计⼊ 98.9 亿美元法律和解费⽤，剔除该费⽤后，每股收益为
7.40 美元。

与此同时，Meta 将 2026 年资本开⽀指引下限从此前的 1300 亿美元上调⾄ 1350 亿美元，上限维
持 1450 亿美元不变。
Meta 盘后⼀度上涨 6% ，后有所回落，截⾄发稿上涨 2.2% 。
⾃由现⾦流转负仍上调资本开⽀下限，新股融资⽀撑 AI 军备竞赛
Meta ⾃由现⾦流⾃上市以来⾸次转负，报告期内为负 22 亿美元，但好于分析师此前预期的负 94
亿美元。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⻅闻历 搜索

### Page 2

公司正将数百亿美元投⼊芯⽚和数据中⼼建设，以期在与 OpenAI 、 Anthropic 的竞争中占据更有
利位置，这⼀投⼊持续消耗其历史上较为充裕的现⾦流。
第三季度， Meta 资本开⽀（包括融资租赁本⾦偿付）达到 349.1 亿美元。公司将全年资本开⽀指
引调整⾄ 1350 亿⾄ 1450 亿美元，此前下限为 1300 亿美元，反映出其仍在为 AI 基础设施扩张投资
⼤量资⾦。
公司宣布创设新的⽆投票权 C 类普通股，计划于 2027 年 1 ⽉⽣效。这⼀安排旨在为公司的 AI 投资
提供更多资⾦来源，同时避免削弱 CEO 扎克伯格的投票控制权。
同时，这种新类别的股票将通过股票股利的形式发放给现有投资者。 Meta 表⽰，持有⼈每持有
⼀股将获得⼀股新股，这实质上等同于⼀次 “ ⼀拆⼆ ” 拆股。
Meta 发⾏⽆投票权新股，换取扎克伯格承诺留居加州
Meta 与扎克伯格达成协议，将向其发⾏⽆投票权 C 类股票。作为交换，扎克伯格承诺未来⼋年
继续居住在加州或 Meta 总部所在的其他司法辖区。

这项安排也与加州拟议中的亿万富豪财富税有关。扎克伯格曾表⽰，相关税收可能迫使其出售
具有投票权的 B 类股票以筹集税款，使其在公司的投票权⽐例降⾄ 53% 以下。
根据协议，扎克伯格可以⽴即出售所获 C 类股票的 20% ，相当于其整体经济权益的 10% ，其余股
份的出售限制将在⼋年内逐步解除。
M e t a 特 别 委 员 会 称 ， 确 保 扎 克 伯 格 在 公 司 总 部 办 公 ⾄ 关 重 要 ， 尤 其 是 在 公 司 与 M e t a
Superintelligence Labs 密切合作、 AI 竞争⽇益激烈之际。
Meta ⾸席财务官 Susan Li 则表⽰， C 类股票主要作为融资⼯具，帮助公司更灵活地为 AI 业务筹
资，同时避免稀释现有股东的投票权。
⼴告业务保持增⻓，第四季度营收指引超预期
⼴告业务仍是 Meta 业绩增⻓的主要⽀撑。第三季度，公司⼴告展⽰量同⽐增⻓ 10% ，平均⼴告
价格上涨 14% 。家庭⽇活跃⼈数达到 36.4 亿，同⽐增⻓ 3% 。

Meta 预计第四季度营收为 730 亿⾄ 760 亿美元，区间中值为 745 亿美元，较上年同期增⻓约
24% 。该指引⾼于 LSEG 汇总的分析师平均预期 730 亿美元左右，显⽰公司对年末⼴告需求和业
务增⻓保持信⼼。
⼴告业务的增⻓也为 AI 投资提供了重要⽀撑。 Meta 正将 AI 应⽤于⼴告投放和定向等环节，同时
尝试通过新的 AI 产品拓展⼴告以外的收⼊来源。不过，新业务的商业化进展能否匹配基础设施投
⼊，仍有待后续业绩验证。
AI 助⼿ Muse 成商业化核⼼，但障碍犹存
Meta 将消费者 AI 助⼿ Muse 定位为整体战略的核⼼，并围绕负责该产品的 Meta 超级智能实验室对
公司架构进⾏重组，同时宣布推出⾯向企业客⼾销售 AI ⼯具的新业务线。
据市场情报机构 SensorTower 数据， Muse ⾃ 9 ⽉上线以来下载量已超过 1000 万次，该应⽤提供
每⽉ 20 美元和 100 美元两档订阅⽅案。

法律和解费⽤拖累利润，成本增⻓值得关注
Meta 第三季度净利润为 98.6 亿美元，摊薄后每股收益为 3.83 美元。公司计⼊ 98.9 亿美元法律和
解费⽤，导致报告利润与剔除⼀次性费⽤后的盈利⽔平出现明显差异。
第三季度营收⾼于市场预期，但报告每股收益低于分析师预期。剔除上述法律和解费⽤后，每

股收益达到 7.40 美元，超过市场预期。
今年 8 ⽉， Meta 与美国近 48 个州达成和解，同意在⼗年内⽀付最⾼ 180 亿美元，并承诺对未成年
⽤⼾使⽤ Facebook 和 Instagram 的⽅式作出⼀系列限制，指控称 Meta 平台被刻意设计以使⼉童
成瘾。
在本季度财报发布当天，新墨西哥州⼀名法官裁定 Meta ⽀付 250 亿美元罚款，陪审团认定 Meta
就其与第三⽅共享⽤⼾数据的⽅式及平台仇恨⾔论和虚假信息政策执⾏情况存在误导⾏为，该
案涉及英国政治咨询公司剑桥分析。
不过，费⽤压⼒并不完全来⾃单⼀法律事项。 Meta 第三季度总成本与费⽤同⽐增⻓ 68% ，达到
516.1 亿美元，明显快于 26% 的营收增幅。营业利润率由上年同期的 40% 降⾄ 20% ，反映出公司
在扩⼤ AI 投⼊的同时，盈利能⼒受到挤压。
Meta 预计 2026 年全年总费⽤将达到 1750 亿⾄ 1790 亿美元。随着 AI 相关⽀出继续增⻓，投资者需
要同时衡量⼴告业务的增⻓、成本扩张速度以及现⾦流变化，⽽不能仅凭剔除⼀次性费⽤后的

每股收益判断经营表现。

## Document 354: Q3 China GDP above expectations, but downside risks remain (2026-10-22)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Q3 China GDP above expectations, but downside risks remain (2026-10-22).pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#china #asia #aud
Q3 China GDP above expectations, but downside risks remain
By Elliot Clarke
Head of International Economics, Westpac
16:03 October 19 2026 Share
Stimulus is required if success in 2026 is to be sustained through 2027 and 2028.
China GDP surprised to the upside in Q3 2026, quarterly growth accelerating from 0.9% in Q2 to 1.2%
in Q3. Annual growth lifted from 4.3% to 4.5% as a result; though, on a year-to-date basis, growth
edged down 0.1ppts to 4.6%. The Q3 result keeps authorities on track to achieve their 4.5-5.0%ytd

ambition for 2026, and also provides a little more time to accelerate domestic demand into 2027.

### Page 2

Broad-based stimulus is required because net exports’ contribution to growth will likely abate in coming
quarters now that the trade surplus is back at record highs, and as many Chinese firms are
increasingly splitting their focus between expanding domestic capacity and offshore production
networks.

While annual retail sales growth accelerated from 0.4% to 1.1% in September, the Q3 year-to-date
growth pulse of 1.1% is just a quarter of the gain reported a year ago. Automobiles, construction
materials and jewellery have been particular heavy detractors over the past year, but most of the other
sub-categories also lack momentum. As we detailed in our September Market Outlook, this is not
because households are cash constrained, but rather as sentiment is extremely weak and the outlook
for employment and wealth highly uncertain. Key for wealth is that house prices for new and
established homes continue to decline month-on-month (respectively 0.1% and 0.3% in September)
five years into the downturn. Property investment’s ongoing decline (-20.6%ytd) suggests this
persistent weakness in sales and prices is demand driven.

While total fixed asset investment has been an outright negative for annual GDP growth since April, a
recovery is not beyond either the public or private sector’s capacity. Rather, across the economy,
entities are waiting for direction and incentive from the Central Government. This is likely to come by
early-2027 to offset the loss of support from trade, and as downside risks to global growth become
more apparent. Helpfully, industrial production continues to grow at a robust pace, 5.3%ytd, borrowing
rates for businesses and households are low, and authorities are beginning to provide banks with
additional lending capacity. For larger businesses, anecdotes suggest officials are also encouraging
major institutional investors to increase their structural allocation to domestic equities, building lasting 
appetite for new equity issuance, which in turn will fund future waves of real economic capacity and
income generation.

We remain comfortable with the view that Chinese GDP growth will hold at or slightly above 4.5%
through 2026–2028. But, the longer the Central Government waits to trigger new real investment
across both the private and public sectors, the higher the likelihood of poor sentiment becoming
entrenched and downside risks crystallising. If authorities wait until after net exports’ contribution has
faded, even holding annual aggregate growth above 4.0% could become a significant challenge. For
this reason, we expect material stimulus initiatives to be announced in the next 3 to 6 months to reset
the property sector and turn the business investment trend.

報會議， FY3Q26 營收 146 億美元 (QoQ+87% ，YoY+ 161 %)，優於市
場共識 129 億美元， GAAP 毛利率 62.4% ，EPS 0.35 美元，顯著優於
市場共識 0.15 美元，且由虧轉盈。公司本次維持 2026 年底年化經常
性收入 (ARR) 目標 1,000 億美元不變，並給予 1Q27 營收目標為
330~370 億美元，大幅優於市場共識 244 億美元，公司優異財報及財
測表現使盤後股價上漲 8%。 
SI(AI) 部門成為第一大業務，且轉虧為盈： SI 部門 FY3Q26 營收 76
億美元，首次成為公司最大業務，主要受惠雲端服務合約及 Grok Bot
帶動。其中 SI 解決方案與基礎建設業務營收 70 億美元，廣告營收
5.4 億美元 (YoY+19%) 。部門調整後 EBITDA 為 50 億美元，營業利
潤 4.31 億美元，由 FY2Q26 營業虧損 13 億美元轉虧為盈。公司目前
算力規模已達 2.2 GW ，預期年底將達 2.5~3GW ，目前每月上線約 20
萬台 GB300 ，2027 年目標則為 13 GW ，將以 Vera Rubin 為主，預期
FY2Q27 末將上線 44 萬台 VR 伺服器。  
Starlink 用 戶數每年高速成長： Connectivity 部門 FY3Q26 營收 52
億美元 (YoY+69% ，QoQ+22%) ，毛利率 55% ，FY3Q26 消費用戶數
達 1,360 萬，淨增加 160 萬用戶。 ARPU(Average Revenue Per User)
方面， FY3Q26 ARPU 69 美元 /月(QoQ+5%) ，低於 FY3Q25 的 83 美
元/月，主因為公司服務範圍由原先歐美轉向南美、非洲、東南亞等
地區，因此在定價策略上進行調整。 Starlink Mobile 手機直連業務月
活躍 用戶突破 2,200 萬(QoQ+37%) ，已獲 FCC 批准發射 15,000 顆
Mobile V2 衛星並收購低頻段頻譜，預計 2028 年初開通服務。  
評價： 我們認為 SpaceX 於太空產業位處龍頭地位，且目前並未有競
爭對手，加上 Grok 帶來強勁成長動能 ，因此 首次 評等 給予「買進」，
評價面考量公司位於高速成長初期，加上公司業務多為訂閱模式，
以 PS 方式給予評價 ，目標價 220 美元(2027 每股營收 10.8 美元 × 20
倍 PSR)。風險因子 :低軌衛星用戶數不如預期、 AI 資料中心建置需
求轉弱 。

SpaceX 為全球 太空產業龍頭 ：SpaceX 業務主要分為 Connectivity 、AI
及 Space 三大部門。 Connectivity 以 Starlink 衛星連網服務為核心，涵
蓋消費者、企業及政府客戶業務，包括 Starshield 相關服務； SI(Super 
Intelligence) 部門以 SpaceXSI( 前 xAI) 為核心，涵蓋 AI 運算服務、 Grok
及 X(前 Twitter) 平台 ；Space 部門則涵蓋 火箭 發射服務與太空計畫開發
業務。公司 FY3Q26 業務比重分別為 Connectivity 36%、SI 52%、Space 
12% 。 Space X 在衛星通訊與火箭發射領域均具領先地位 ， 2025 年
SpaceX 共發射 165 次火箭， 全 球 火 箭 發 射共 325 次， 其 市 占 率 約
50.8% ，而在衛星通訊領域， Starlink 則為目前唯一可提供寬頻衛星通
訊服務給大規模用戶的供應商。  
圖表一：衛星產業市場規模

資料來源： SIA ，中信投顧整理  
Falcon 9 開啟火箭技術革命 ： 傳統 火箭多為一次性 設計 ，主要結構於
發 射後 拋棄， 使每 次發射 任務 均須重 新製 造與整 備， 導致 火 箭發 射成
本高昂。而 SpaceX 的獵鷹 9 號(Falcon 9 )於 2015 年首次完成一級火箭
回收，並於 2017 年實現回收助推器再次飛行，突破火箭一次性使用限
制，降低火箭發射成本， 使衛星寬頻通訊商業化可行性提高。 Falcon 9
每次發射衛星 報價 約在 3,200 美元 /公斤 ，SpaceX 自用成本更低 ，而最
新 的傳 統火 箭(Ariane 6) 發射 報價 則 約在 6,000 美元 /公斤 ， 相比 之 下
SpaceX 在火箭發射成本上有顯著優勢。 而 下 一 代 火 箭星艦 (Starship)
推測每公斤成本約在 600 美元 /公斤， 且目標 為全部回收並 將發射成本
進一步降低至 200 美元 /公斤 ，有望進一步帶動衛星發射數量 。

運載空間 300立方公尺 200立方公尺 1,000立方公尺

空計畫開發業務， FY3Q26 營收 18 億美元 (QoQ+84% ，YoY+6%) ，上

射羅馬太空望遠鏡及 11 次客戶任務 )，並獲得多項發射任務合約 (3 項
Dragon 載人 任務 、Artemis III Starlink 合約、 18 項國 安 發射 任務 、
Starship 登月合約及 3 項 Starfall 任務 )。公司先 前宣佈設立路易斯安那
州火箭基地，預期將有 20 座以上發射塔，每年可發射數百萬噸物資進
入太空，將是 Starship 主要發射基地 ，預期 2028~2029 年可實現首次
發射。此外 Starship 目標於 2027 上半年每週 發射一次 、下半年每週 發
射兩次 ，2027 年初將進行二級 火箭 再飛及在軌燃料加注 ，並開始發射
Mobile V2 及 Starmind 衛星。

## Document 371: The Commodities Feed_ Attacks on Saudi Arabia fan oil supply worries

Source PDF: all report pdf/The Commodities Feed_ Attacks on Saudi Arabia fan oil supply worries.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-22
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Oil prices remain well-supported after fresh attacks on Saudi
Arabia, with traders focused on risks to the outlook rather than
the resilience of Persian Gulf supplies

Warren Patterson and Ewa Manthey
Source: Shutterstock
Energy - Kuwait pumping more than 2m b/d
Oil prices remain well bid, with ICE Brent back above $110/bbl this
morning following additional deadly attacks on Saudi Arabia.
Drones struck the Shaybah oilﬁeld and Dammam's international
airport. An oil processing plant was the target of the attack. Saudi
Arabia’s energy ministry said production hasn’t been impacted. The
escalation prompted the US State Department to order non-
emergency US government personnel to leave the country. This will
concern markets, suggesting little improvement in Middle East
tensions, and possibly further escalation.
Despite the volatile environment, oil ﬂows from the Persian Gulf are
holding up. According to the CEO of Kuwait Petroleum Corp, Kuwait

### Page 2

is exporting as much crude oil as before the war and producing
more than 2m b/d. However, he admitted that the shipping of
reﬁned products is more of an issue. While oil ﬂows have remained
elevated, President Trump has expressed concern that oil prices
remain elevated due to supply risks. This leaves plenty of
uncertainty over the sustainability of ﬂows. The sizeable risk
premium currently embedded in prices is unlikely to fade until clear
signs of de-escalation emerge and reassure investors that regional

supply will remain uninterrupted.
EIA inventory data shows US crude oil inventories fell by 3.06m
barrels over the last week. There was an additional 300k-barrel fall
in Strategic Petroleum Reserves. The data still reﬂects the impact of
hurricane activity in the US Gulf of Mexico, with US oil production
estimated to have fallen 410k b/d week-on-week. Crude oil imports
fell by 291k b/d, while exports fell by 1.13m b/d. For reﬁned
products, implied gasoline demand rose 574k b/d WoW. This

contributed to the 2.95m barrel decline in gasoline inventories.
However, the diesel market saw some relief, with distillate stocks
rising by 1.68m barrels. Total distillate stocks stood at just over
104m barrels, a seasonal low dating back 30 years.
Metals — Indonesia nickel output cuts
LME nickel prices gained signiﬁcantly yesterday, settling 3.65%
higher on coordinated production cuts in Indonesia. Major Chinese
nickel producers operating in Indonesia plan to curb production to

support weak nickel prices. Under the agreement, reﬁneries would
halve production of mixed hydroxide precipitate (MHP), a key
battery feedstock. Nickel pig iron (NPI) output for stainless steel
production would be reduced by about 20%. A similar proposal
failed to reach consensus in August, but persistent price weakness
increased pressure on producers to take action.
Emirates Global Aluminium, the Middle East’s largest aluminium
producer, has restored half capacity at its Al Taweelah smelter in

Abu Dhabi after damage sustained amid the Iran war. The
company remains on track to fully restore operations by the ﬁrst
quarter next year. However, a complete recovery in sales will also

Agriculture — Wheat extends losses on

ceaseﬁre hopes
CBOT wheat prices fell for a ﬁfth consecutive session, settling 2.33%
lower yesterday. Prices fell after Trump called for a Ukraine-Russia
peace agreement, easing the geopolitical risk premium. After talks
with US oﬃcials, Volodymyr Zelensky adopted a more conciliatory
tone, while Vladimir Putin also met with a US envoy. No tangible
progress was announced. A ceaseﬁre could boost grain exports
from both countries, improving global supply and weighing on

prices.

Past performance is not a reliable indicator of future performance. The forecasts given above are predictive in character. Whilst every effort has been taken to ensure that the assumptions on which the forecasts are based are reasonable, 
the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
Markets have had little respite over the past month. Hopes 
of a lasting US–Iran agreement have faded and intermittent 
attacks on shipping have kept Brent oil prices in triple digits. 
Workarounds have allowed a meaningful share of crude to 
continue flowing but these arrangements are slower and 
costlier, while depleted inventories leave little room to 
absorb another shock and a range of disruptions to refining 
and shipping are also impacting downstream energy costs. 
Bond markets have been just as restless, with continued lifts 
in government bond yields across much of the globe and 
more sharply in the US. 
Notably, oil is no longer doing the heavy lifting for yields. 
The rise has been concentrated in real yields, with the Fed’s 
September hike – and its signal of more to come – acting as 
something of a trigger. In the US, fiscal concerns have been 
more of a sub-plot, though the Treasury’s efforts to hold 
down long yields have arguably only drawn more attention 
to them. 
In our view, markets are getting ahead of themselves on 
monetary policy offshore. We expect just one more hike 
from the Fed rather than the three the market currently has 
priced, with stretched housing affordability posing risks to 
both consumption and inflation. Central banks in Europe, the 
UK and Canada are also likely to bide their time after a final 
move. Europe is weathering the energy shock better than 
feared and has far more fiscal room to respond than the US – 
one reason we still see the US dollar trending lower over the 
next two years.
The RBA hiked again in September and looks set to follow 
up in November, taking the cash rate to a new cycle high. 
Some of the risks the Board flagged have crystallised, 
but the minutes also reveal a tendency to reach for the 
more hawkish read of the data, in addition to the familiar 
downbeat take on supply capacity. Our base case assumes 
no return to full-scale conflict, but with energy buffers 
depleted, bond markets less forgiving and the RBA already 
leaning hawkish, the margin for error – for markets and 
policymakers alike – is starting to ‘wear thin’.
Australia: After a soft but better than expected first half, the 
Australian economy is facing into more challenges as another 
round of disruptions from global energy markets combine with 
further interest rate rises and a deepening housing downturn. 
The demand mix should see capacity pressures continue to 
ease but with significant risks on either side.
Middle East: Low inventories and higher shipping costs will 
keep oil and fuel prices elevated, with Brent futures forecast 
to average US$101/bbl in Q4 before easing to US$77/bbl by 
Q4 2027 as supply recovers. Risks are two-sided: escalation 
could push Brent towards US$150/bbl, while an early US-Iran 
agreement could see a faster normalisation in supply.
Commodities: Most commodities rose in September, with 
many of the themes that drove moves across August remaining 
in place. Japanese LNG and Brent oil recorded strong gains 
amid Middle East tensions. Copper and aluminium extended 
recent gains while iron ore was supported by higher freight 
and production costs. Gold fell after the Fed’s September rate 
hike. 
Global FX Markets: In late September and early-October 
the DXY index popped higher to near 103, a high back to 
early-2025. Underlying the move are expectations that 
inflation will prove persistent globally as well as a belief that 
the US’ tech sector leaves it best positioned to weather the 
storm.
New Zealand: Higher fuel costs and a weaker exchange rate 
have lifted the short-term inflation outlook while the economy 
has continued to grow through the Iran war. Growth looks set 
to continue to be buoyed by strong commodity prices and a 
supportive exchange rate. However, downside risks are growing 
from higher energy costs and long-term interest rates. 
United States: The US housing market remains under 
considerable pressure. Prices continue to edge up, supporting 
wealth. But up-and-coming main street consumers are 
struggling with affordability and availability, posing enduring 
risks for both consumption growth and inflation.   
China: With each month that passes, the risk of disappointing 
authorities’ 4.5-5.0% ambition grows. While the Government 
is right to take a targeted approach to support, it must quickly 
multiply its scale if downside risks are to be kept at bay. 
Asia: For much of the past twenty years, Asia has been a 
source of disinflation for the world. But a combination of ageing 
populations, rising labour costs, increasing electricity demand 
and technology sector developments imply Asia may provide 
less support to global disinflation over the decade ahead.
Europe & UK: The euro has come under considerable pressure 
during the past month. That said, the European economy 
continues to tick along nicely while inflation risks remain 
manageable, allowing the ECB to be measured in their response.

Wearing thin

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FINANCIAL MARKETS
The main development affecting global markets this month
has been the sharp lift in selected bond yields, especially in
the US and France. Since the previous Market Outlook was
published, 10-year benchmarks have risen 27bps for US

Treasuries, to be well above the 5% threshold, and 40bps for
French bonds. The latter move contrasts with German bunds,
which have seen little change in yields at that tenor over the
month, though they are up more than 60bps year to date.
Several factors are behind the latest rise, with no single
cause dominating. For some of these factors, the underlying
conditions had been in evidence for some time, but recent
events focused attention on them. The rise has been primarily

a story of real yields: longer-run inflation expectations implied
by both breakevens and swaps have not increased that much.
For much of the year, fluctuations in oil prices – and thus the
near-term inflation outlook – fed into market views of central
bank policy, and longer yields as well. More recently, though,
this correlation has broken down. Shifts in expectations about
central bank policy have remained important for bond pricing,
as evident in the concentration of the increase in real yields.

The FOMC’s decision to increase the Fed funds rate, and flag in
the ‘dot plots’ that at least one more hike was likely, seems to
have been something of a trigger event for repricing, not only
for US yields. Also salient were the various actions taken by
the US Treasury to suppress long yields and improve the US
government’s debt-servicing position. These efforts had little
lasting effect and yields are now higher, and higher relative to
peers, than before. But they may have served to draw attention

to the growing issues around US fiscal sustainability. High
supply of debt from hyperscaler firms to fund the AI / data
centre boom has also likely pressured yields at the margin.
That said, fiscal concerns are unlikely to have been the main
issue driving the lift in US yields. The slope of the US yield
curve is noticeably flatter than at the beginning of the year;
in other words, short yields have risen even more than the
10-year yield, signifying tighter expected monetary policy

lifting yields across the curve. Another lens on this shift is that
2-year and other short-dated yields have increased more than
estimates of risk-neutral rate, such as that from the NY Fed.
Fiscal concerns have been more salient in the case of France,
where spreads to bunds have widened from around 70bps at
the beginning of the year to nearly 140bps currently, wider than
Greece or Italy, with about 40bps of the widening occurring in
the past month. Contagion to other euro area sovereigns has

been limited, with spreads widening around 22bps over the
month for Greece, Italy and Belgium, and 13bps for Spain.
Many factors lifting global bond yields ...
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
